\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{{{(A{\downarrow}R)}}}
\textstyle{(C,\eta)}
\textstyle{(D,g)}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{{{(A{\downarrow}R)}}}

My favorite way — a syntax sugar! — of visualizing the comma
category
{(A{\downarrow}R)}
is the middle third of the diagram above, in which
the objects of
{(A{\downarrow}R)}
are depicted as L-shaped diagrams. To
understand the typings and the commutativity conditions we have to
look at the left third — it indicates that
f
must obey
Rf\circ\eta=g
.
The right third shows a generic morphism in
{(A{\downarrow}R)}
without the syntax
sugar, but we still have to look at the left third to type it. We
have:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\text{In a context in which}&\mathbf{A}\text{ is a category},\\
&\mathbf{B}\text{ is a category},\\
&R:\mathbf{B}\rightarrow\mathbf{A},\\
&A\text{ is an object of $\mathbf{A}$},\\
\text{we define the category}&{(A{\downarrow}R)}\text{ as follows:}\\[5.0pt]
\text{An object of}&{(A{\downarrow}R)}\\
\text{is a pair}&(C,\eta)\\
\text{in which}&C:\mathbf{B}_{0}\\
\text{and}&\eta:\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{A}}(A,RC);\\
\text{so}&(C,\eta):(C{:}\mathbf{B}_{0})\times\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{A}}(A,RC)\\
\text{and}&{(A{\downarrow}R)}_{0}:=(C{:}\mathbf{B}_{0})\times\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{A}}(A,RC).\\[5.0pt]
\text{A morphism}&f:(C,\eta)\rightarrow(D,g)\text{ in ${(A{\downarrow}R)}$}\\
\text{is an}&f:\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{B}}(C,D)\text{ such that $Rf\circ\eta=g$},\\
\text{or equivalently a pair}&(f,\langle\!\langle Rf\circ\eta=g\rangle\!\rangle);\\
\text{we have}&(f,\langle\!\langle Rf\circ\eta=g\rangle\!\rangle):(f{:}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{B}}(C,D))\times\llbracket Rf\circ\eta=g\rrbracket,\\
\text{so}&\operatorname{Hom}_{(A{\downarrow}R)}((C,\eta),(D,g)):=\\
&(f{:}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{B}}(C,D))\times\llbracket Rf\circ\eta=g\rrbracket.\end{array}

This defines formally the first two components of the category

{(A{\downarrow}R)}
. Remember that a category
\mathbf{C}
has seven components:

\mathbf{C}=(\mathbf{C}_{0},\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{C}},\mathrm{id}_{\mathbf{C}},\circ_{\mathbf{C}};\mathsf{assoc}_{\mathbf{C}},\mathsf{idL}_{\mathbf{C}},\mathsf{idR}_{\mathbf{C}})

We are pretending that the other components of
{(A{\downarrow}R)}
are “obvious”
in the sense of Section
3
. Note the we used the
notations for dependent types and witnesses of the sections

6.1
and
6.2
.

8.2
Universalness as something extra

We can consider that an universal arrow is an arrow
\eta:A\rightarrow RC
with
something extra. We saw how to represent this “something extra” in
Type Theory: a universal arrow
\eta
is a pair
(\eta,\mathsf{univ}_{\eta})
, where

\mathsf{univ}_{\eta}
is its “universalness”, that we defined in one way in
sec.
4.2
and in another way in
sec.
6.6
.

Universalness is just one ‘-ness’ among many. Several of these
“-ness”es have standard graphical representations: for example
pullbackness is indicated by a ‘
’, and monicness is
indicated by a tail like this: ‘
\rightarrowtail
’.
[
FS90
]

defines lots of graphical representations for “-ness”es starting on
its page 37. We will sometimes use an ‘
:=
’ to define a new
annotation that is an abbreviation for extra structure:

\textstyle{A}

8.3
Opposite categories

Suppose that we have a diagram
A\overset{f}{\rightarrow}B\overset{g}{\rightarrow}C
in a category

\mathbf{A}
. There are several different notations for the corresponding
diagram in
{\mathbf{A}^{\operatorname{op}}}
: for example, in
[
CWM
, p.33]
it would be
written as
A\overset{f^{\operatorname{op}}}{\leftarrow}B\overset{g^{\operatorname{op}}}{\leftarrow}C
, while in

[
AT11
, p.15]
as
A\overset{f}{\leftarrow}B\overset{g}{\leftarrow}C
. The
convention (COT) says that the notation in our diagrams should be as
close as possible to the notation in the original text — so let’s
see how to support the notation in
[
AT11
]
, that
looks a bit harder than the one in
[
CWM
]
.

We want to define a new category,
{\mathbf{A}^{\operatorname{op}}}
, using tricks similar to the
ones in Section
8.1
, but now we can’t pretend that
the new composition is obvious. We will define
({\mathbf{A}^{\operatorname{op}}})_{0}
,

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{A}^{\operatorname{op}}}
,
\mathrm{id}_{\mathbf{A}^{\operatorname{op}}}
, and
\circ_{\mathbf{A}^{\operatorname{op}}}
without any textual
explanations, with just the diagrams to convince the readed that our
definitions are reasonable.

In the diagram below
F:\mathbf{A}^{\operatorname{op}}\rightarrow\mathbf{B}
is a contravariant functor,
and the
\mathbf{A}
above
\mathbf{A}^{\operatorname{op}}
indicates that
g:C\rightarrow D
is a morphism
of
\mathbf{A}
, not of
\mathbf{A}^{\operatorname{op}}
. I am not very happy with this trick
but I haven’t found a better alternative yet.

8.4
The Yoneda Lemma

The formalization of
\mathsf{Y0}
as a series of typings and definitions
in Section
7.3

suggests
that
some

operations from Type Theory that can be applied on the formalization
side should be translatable to the diagram side; for example,
substitution. This one clearly works: if we substitute
\mathbf{A}
by

\mathbf{Set}
and
A
by the set 1 we get this,

\mathsf{Y0}\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{A}:=\mathbf{Set}\\
A:=1\end{matrix}\right]\qquad=\quad\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 17.68817pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern 54.46704pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern For each object
S
of
\mathbf{Set}
we have a bijection between elements of

S
and morphisms
1\rightarrow S
. We will denote the morphism from 1 to
S

that “chooses” an element
s\in S
by
\ulcorner s\urcorner
; the pronounciation
of
\ulcorner s\urcorner
is “the name of
s
”. We have a bijection between
‘
s
’s and
\ulcorner s\urcorner
s:

For each
D\in\mathbf{B}
we have a bijection
\mathbf{Set}(1,RD)\leftrightarrow RD
— and we
can use these bijections to build a natural isomorphism

\mathbf{Set}(1,R-)\leftrightarrow R
. We will draw it vertically, and complete the
triangle:

\mathsf{Y1}\qquad:=\qquad\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 17.68817pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern 54.46704pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}}}}}

We can use that natural isomorphism to obtain ‘
\beta
’s from ‘
\alpha
’s, and
vice-versa, by composition. We could draw an arrow for the bijection
between ‘
\alpha
’s and ‘
\beta
’s and another arrow for the bijection between
‘
\eta
’s and elements of
RC
, but we will prefer to omit them.

We will call the diagram above
\mathsf{Y1}
. It doesn’t have a single
top-level arrow, so we can’t apply the convention (CTL) to it, and we
need to specify its “meaning” explicitly. We will consider that its
three bijections are top-level objects, and so the diagram
\mathsf{Y1}

says that we have these bijections:

\begin{array}[]{l}RC\\
\leftrightarrow\;\mathbf{Set}(1,RC)\\
\leftrightarrow\;\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{B}}(\mathbf{B}(C,-),\mathbf{Set}(1,R-))\\
\leftrightarrow\;\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{B}}(\mathbf{B}(C,-),R)\\
\end{array}

The Yoneda Lemma “is” the bijection
RC\leftrightarrow\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{B}}(\mathbf{B}(C,-),R)

— check how it is defined in
[
Leinster
, thm.4.2.1]
,

[
Riehl
, thm.2.2.4]
,
[
CWM
, p.61]
,
[
Awo06
, lemma 8.2]
.
Some books show how to calculate the element of
RC
associated to a
given
\beta
and vice-versa, and most treat
\mathbf{Set}(1,R-)
as something
secondary. If we represent this idea of the Yoneda Lemma in the same
format the we used in sec.
7.3
, we get this:

\mathsf{Y2}\;\;:=\;\;\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 17.68817pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern 39.47528pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 44.97528pt\raise-22.6502pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 33.90935pt\raise-52.47116pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-17.68817pt\raise-74.9588pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbf{B}(C,-)}$}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 38.14021pt\raise-104.94232pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{R}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 16.28067pt\raise-97.78552pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\beta}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 38.14021pt\raise-100.39006pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}}}}}\qquad\begin{array}[]{rl}&\mathbf{B}\text{ is a category},\\
&R:\mathbf{B}\rightarrow\mathbf{Set},\\
&C\in\mathbf{B},\\[5.0pt]
&\mathbf{B}(C,-):\mathbf{B}\rightarrow\mathbf{Set},\\
&\mathbf{B}(C,-)_{0}:=\lambda D.\mathbf{B}(C,D),\\
&\mathbf{B}(C,-)_{1}:=\lambda g.\lambda f.\;g\circ f,\\[5.0pt]
&e\in RC,\\
&\ulcorner e\urcorner:=\lambda{*}.e,\\
&\beta:\mathbf{B}(C,-)\rightarrow R,\\[5.0pt]
&(e\mapsto\beta_{0}):=\lambda e.\lambda D.\lambda f.\;Rf(e),\\
&(\beta\mapsto e):=\lambda\beta.\beta C\mathrm{id}_{C},\\
\end{array}

8.5
The Yoneda embedding

Let’s define
\mathsf{Y3}
as the result of this substituion:

\mathsf{Y3}\;\;=\;\;\mathsf{Y2}\left[\begin{matrix}R:=\mathbf{B}(B,-)\\
e:=\varphi\\
\end{matrix}\right]

We have:

\mathsf{Y3}\;\;=\;\;\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 17.68817pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern 39.47528pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern and

can be simplified, and we can
derive this other diagram from it:

3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathbf{B}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-15.99307pt\raise-74.9588pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\phantom{{}^{\operatorname{op}}}\mathbf{B}^{\operatorname{op}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 33.90935pt\raise-74.9588pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbf{Set}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 19.56163pt\raise-69.7227pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.875pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{y}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 33.90935pt\raise-74.9588pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}}}}}\qquad\begin{array}[]{rl}&\mathbf{B}\text{ is a category},\\
&B\in\mathbf{B},\\
&C\in\mathbf{B},\\[5.0pt]
&\varphi:B\rightarrow C,\\
&\beta:\mathbf{B}(C,-)\rightarrow\mathbf{B}(B,-),\\
&(\varphi\mapsto\beta_{0}):=\lambda\varphi.\lambda D.\lambda f.\;f\circ\varphi,\\
&(\beta\mapsto\varphi):=\lambda\beta.\beta C\mathrm{id}_{C},\\
\end{array}

Let’s call it
\mathsf{Y4}
. This is one of the
Yoneda Embeddings
;
compare that diagram with the ones in
[
Riehl
, p.60]
. In

[
Och22
]
we show how to formalize it in Agda as a corollary
of
\mathsf{Y0}
,
\mathsf{Y1}
,
\mathsf{Y2}
, and
\mathsf{Y3}
.

Note that the functor
C\mapsto\mathbf{B}(C,-)
in
\mathsf{Y4}
is
contravariant, and we used the trick from
sec.
8.3
to indicate this.

8.6
Representable functors

Some books, like
[
Leinster
]
and
[
Riehl
]
, present
representable functors first, then lots of examples, and only then
they present the Yoneda Lemma. In our diagram
\mathsf{Y1}
a
representation
for the functor
R
is a pair
(C,\beta)
such that the
natural transformation
\beta
is a natural iso. It is easy to see that we
have these bijections, or bi-implications:

\begin{array}[]{l}\text{natural iso-ness of $\beta$}\\
\leftrightarrow\;\text{natural iso-ness of $\alpha$}\\
\leftrightarrow\;\text{universalness of $\eta$}\\
\end{array}

The last one holds in the diagram
\mathsf{Y0}
too.

Many of the textbook examples of representable functors are
consequences of a simple theorem that shows that every functor

R:\mathbf{B}\rightarrow\mathbf{Set}
with a left adjoint is representable. The diagram

\mathsf{Y5}
below is a skeleton for a proof of that theorem:

\mathsf{Y5}\;\;:=\;\;\;\;\;\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 10.15279pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern 181.897pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{1}$}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 174.65913pt\raise-29.98352pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{RL1}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 187.397pt\raise-14.0751pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\begin{smallmatrix}\eta_{1}\\
univ\end{smallmatrix}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 187.397pt\raise-22.6502pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 133.51894pt\raise-29.98352pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{L1}$}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 174.65913pt\raise-29.98352pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{RL1}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 151.32452pt\raise-29.98352pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise The unit arrow
\eta_{1}:1\rightarrow RL1
is universal — note the ‘
\mathsf{univ}
’ in the
upper right arrow — and so its associated ‘
\beta
’ is a natural iso; we
drew it with a ‘
\leftrightarrow
’.

Statements like “the functor
blah
is representable” are very
common in CT texts, and their natural isos is usually written just
like “
R\cong\mathbf{B}(L1,-)
”, without a name. A good way to draw the
missing diagrams for a text should not force us to invent names, and
so we should be allowed to omit the names of arrows when this is
convenient.

This is the example (iv) in
[
Riehl
, p.52]
:

(iv)

The functor
U:\mathbf{Ring}\rightarrow\mathbf{Set}
is represented by the unital
ring
\mathbb{Z}[x]
, the polynomial ring in one variable with integer
coefficients. A unital ring homomorphism
Z[x]\rightarrow R
is uniquely
determined by the image of
x
; put another way,
\mathbb{Z}[x]
is the
free unital ring on a single generator
.

The diagram below is a good way to visualize that:

\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{U\mathbb{Z}[x]}
\scriptstyle{\begin{smallmatrix}\ulcorner x\urcorner\\
\mathsf{univ}\end{smallmatrix}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}[x]}
\textstyle{U\mathbb{Z}[x]}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Ring}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Set}}
\scriptstyle{U}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Ring}(\mathbb{Z}[x],-)}
\textstyle{U}
\textstyle{FA}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{FA}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{UB}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{UB}

\textstyle{\mathbf{Ring}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Set}}
\scriptstyle{F}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Ring}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Set}}
\scriptstyle{U}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{UFA}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{A}}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{U\mathbb{Z}[x]}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{1}}

Its left half is useful for when we want to remember how that
representation is generated by an adjunction of the form
F\dashv U
, but it
can be omitted. In sec.
2
I drew that diagram
without its left half.

8.7
The 2-category of categories

Natural transformations are often drawn as ‘
\Rightarrow
’s in the middle of
“cells” whose walls are functors. If
F,G:\mathbf{A}\rightarrow\mathbf{B}
are functors
and
T:F\rightarrow G
is natural transformation, then
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B},F,G,T

are drawn like this:

\textstyle{\mathbf{A}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{B}}
\scriptstyle{F}
\textstyle{\mathbf{A}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{B}}
\scriptstyle{G}
\textstyle{{\Downarrow\scriptstyle T}}

There are several ways to compose functors and natural transformations
— see
[
Riehl
, section 1.7]
,
[
Haz19
, section A.5]
, and

[
Pow90
]
for the details and the precise terminology. For
example, in

\textstyle{\mathbf{A}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{B}}
\scriptstyle{F}
\textstyle{\mathbf{B}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{C}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle R}
\textstyle{\mathbf{B}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{C}}
\scriptstyle{G}
\textstyle{\mathbf{A}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{C}}
\scriptstyle{H}
\textstyle{{\Downarrow\scriptstyle T}}
\textstyle{{\Downarrow\scriptstyle U}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{A}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{C}}
\scriptstyle{GF}
\textstyle{\mathbf{A}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{C}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle RF}
\textstyle{\mathbf{A}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{C}}
\scriptstyle{H}
\textstyle{{\Downarrow\scriptstyle UF}}
\textstyle{{\Downarrow\scriptstyle T}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{A}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{C}}
\scriptstyle{GF}
\textstyle{\mathbf{A}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{C}}
\scriptstyle{H}
\textstyle{{\Downarrow\scriptstyle T\cdot UF}}
\textstyle{{=}}
\textstyle{{=}}

we used “whiskering” and then “vertical composition”.

We can use internal views to lower the level of abstraction of the
diagrams above. If we draw the images of an object
A\in\mathbf{A}
by the
functors and natural transformations we get:

\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{FA}
\textstyle{FA}
\textstyle{GFA}
\textstyle{FA}
\textstyle{RFA}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{HA}
\textstyle{GFA}
\textstyle{RFA}
\scriptstyle{UFA}
\textstyle{RFA}
\textstyle{HA}
\scriptstyle{TA}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{GFA}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{RFA}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{HA}
\textstyle{GFA}
\textstyle{RFA}
\scriptstyle{UFA}
\textstyle{RFA}
\textstyle{HA}
\scriptstyle{TA}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{GFA}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{HA}
\textstyle{GFA}
\textstyle{HA}
\scriptstyle{\begin{smallmatrix}TA\circ UFA\\
=(T\cdot UF)A\end{smallmatrix}}
\textstyle{{=}}
\textstyle{{=}}

Note that this process of taking a “pasting diagram” and looking at
its internal view — in which many names become longer and some nodes
are duplicated — is the opposite of what people usually do in the
literature: they usually go from pasting diagrams to string diagrams,
in which most names are omitted. See
[
Mar14
]
and

[
Haz19
, section A.5]
.

8.8
Kan extensions

Kan extensions are usually drawn using 2-cells (
[
Riehl
, definition
6.1.1]
), but they can also be drawn as adjunctions
(
[
Riehl
, proposition 6.1.5]
,
[
CWM
, section X.3]
). Let’s see
how to draw them in both ways at the same time in a way that makes the
translation clear. Here is the diagram:

\textstyle{GF}
\textstyle{G}
\textstyle{GF}
\textstyle{H}
\scriptstyle{V}
\textstyle{G}
\textstyle{\mathsf{Ran}_{F}H}
\scriptstyle{U}

\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{\mathsf{Ran}_{F}H}
\textstyle{{=R}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{A}}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\circ F}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{A}}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{Ran}_{F}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{A}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{B}}
\scriptstyle{F}

\textstyle{\mathbf{A}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{B}}
\scriptstyle{F}
\textstyle{\mathbf{B}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{C}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle R}
\textstyle{\mathbf{B}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{C}}
\scriptstyle{\forall G}
\textstyle{\mathbf{A}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{C}}
\scriptstyle{H}
\textstyle{{\Downarrow\scriptstyle T}}
\textstyle{{\Downarrow\scriptstyle\exists!U}}

We will consider right Kan extensions only.

Fix
F:\mathbf{A}\rightarrow\mathbf{B}
and a category
\mathbf{C}
. We have a functor

(\circ F):\mathbf{C}^{\mathbf{B}}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}^{\mathbf{A}}
. Suppose that it has a right adjoint,

(\circ F)\dashv\mathsf{Ran}_{F}
. For each natural transformation
H:\mathbf{A}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}
its
image by
\mathsf{Ran}_{f}
,
R:=\mathsf{Ran}_{F}H
, is a natural transformation

R:\mathbf{B}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}
. We have:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}(\circ F)&\dashv&\mathsf{Ran}_{H}\\
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{C}^{\mathbf{A}}}((\circ F)-,-)&\cong&\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{C}^{\mathbf{B}}}(-,\mathsf{Ran}_{F}-),\\
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{C}^{\mathbf{A}}}((\circ F)G,H)&\cong&\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{C}^{\mathbf{B}}}(G,\mathsf{Ran}_{F}H),\\
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{C}^{\mathbf{A}}}(G\circ F,H)&\cong&\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{C}^{\mathbf{B}}}(G,\mathsf{Ran}_{F}H),\\
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{C}^{\mathbf{A}}}(GF,H)&\cong&\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{C}^{\mathbf{B}}}(G,R)\\
\end{array}

and if we substitute
[G:=R]
in
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{C}^{\mathbf{B}}}(G,R)
and we
transpose
\mathrm{id}_{R}
to the left we obtain a morphism
T:RF\rightarrow H
. The pair

(R,H)
obeys a certain universal property, that we will call
“Ran-ness”:

\forall G.\;\forall V.\;\exists!U.\;(T\cdot UF)=V.

The usual way of defining right Kan extensions is by starting with the
functors
F:\mathbf{A}\rightarrow\mathbf{B}
and
H:\mathbf{A}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}
and then saying that a
pair
(R,T)
is
a
right Kan extension of
H
along
F
iff it
obeys Ran-ness; the functor
\mathsf{Ran}_{F}
and the adjunction come later.
See
[
Riehl
]
, section 6.1.

Note that we don’t draw the ‘
\forall V:GF\rightarrow H
’ in the right half of the
diagram — it would overwrite the rest.

8.9
All concepts are Kan extensions

Both
[
CWM
]
and
[
Riehl
]
have sections called “All concepts
are Kan extensions” — section X.7 in
[
CWM
]
and 6.5 in

[
Riehl
]
. Now that we have a favorite way of drawing right Kan
extensions we can use it to draw diagrams for 1) binary products in

\mathbf{Set}
are right Kan extensions, 2) limits are right Kan extensions
and 3) left adjoints are right Kan extensions. Here they are.

1.

Let
{\bullet\bullet}
be the discrete category with two objects,
{\bullet}
be
the discrete category with one object, and
!:{\bullet\bullet}\rightarrow{\bullet}
be the
unique functor from
{\bullet\bullet}
to
{\bullet}
. Then:

\textstyle{(X,X)}
\textstyle{\forall X}
\textstyle{(X,X)}
\textstyle{(Y{\times}Z,Y{\times}Z)}
\scriptstyle{(h,h)}
\textstyle{\forall X}
\textstyle{Y{\times}Z}
\scriptstyle{\exists!h}

\textstyle{(Y{\times}Z,Y{\times}Z)}
\textstyle{Y{\times}Z}
\textstyle{(Y{\times}Z,Y{\times}Z)}
\textstyle{(Y,Z)}
\scriptstyle{(\pi,\pi^{\prime})}
\textstyle{(X,X)}
\textstyle{(Y,Z)}
\scriptstyle{\forall(f,g)}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Set}^{\bullet\bullet}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Set}^{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{\Delta:=(\circ!)}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Set}^{\bullet\bullet}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Set}^{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{\lim:=\mathsf{Ran}_{!}}
\textstyle{{\bullet\bullet}}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\scriptstyle{!}

\textstyle{{\bullet\bullet}}
\textstyle{{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{!}
\textstyle{{\bullet}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Set}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle Y\times Z}
\textstyle{{\bullet}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Set}}
\scriptstyle{\forall X}
\textstyle{{\bullet\bullet}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Set}}
\scriptstyle{(Y,Z)}
\textstyle{{\Downarrow\scriptstyle(\pi,\pi^{\prime})}}
\textstyle{{\Downarrow\scriptstyle\exists!h}}

2.

Let
\mathbf{I}
be a finite index category — for example,
\mathbf{I}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&&1\\
&&\downarrow\\
2&\rightarrow&3\end{smallmatrix}\right)
— and let
\mathbf{C}
be a category with
finite limits. A functor
D:\mathbf{I}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}
is a diagram of shape

\mathbf{I}
in
\mathbf{C}
. Let’s denote by
\bm{1}
the discrete category
with a single object — the name ‘
\bm{1}
’ is more standard than
‘
\bullet
’. Then:

\textstyle{\Delta X}
\textstyle{\forall X}
\textstyle{\Delta X}
\textstyle{\Delta\lim_{\mathbf{I}}D}
\scriptstyle{\Delta f}
\textstyle{\forall X}
\textstyle{\lim_{\mathbf{I}}D}
\scriptstyle{\exists!f}

\textstyle{\Delta\lim_{\mathbf{I}}D}
\textstyle{\lim_{\mathbf{I}}D}
\textstyle{\Delta\lim_{\mathbf{I}}D}
\textstyle{D}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}
\textstyle{\Delta X}
\textstyle{D}
\scriptstyle{\forall\gamma}
\textstyle{\mathbf{C}^{\mathbf{I}}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{C}^{\bm{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\Delta:=(\circ!)}
\textstyle{\mathbf{C}^{\mathbf{I}}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{C}^{\bm{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lim:=\mathsf{Ran}_{!}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{I}}
\textstyle{\bm{1}}
\scriptstyle{!}

\textstyle{\mathbf{I}}
\textstyle{\bm{1}}
\scriptstyle{!}
\textstyle{\bm{1}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{C}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\lim_{\mathbf{I}}D}
\textstyle{\bm{1}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{C}}
\scriptstyle{\forall X}
\textstyle{\mathbf{I}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{C}}
\scriptstyle{D}
\textstyle{{\Downarrow\scriptstyle\varepsilon}}
\textstyle{{\Downarrow\scriptstyle\exists!f}}

3.

Left adjoints are right Kan extensions. If

\mathbf{B}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.25143pt\raise 7.89168pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.39168pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{L}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 3.96446pt\raise-7.89166pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.39168pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{R}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 19.98901pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbf{A}

is an adjunction, then
(L,\varepsilon)
is a right Kan extension of
\mathrm{id}_{\mathbf{B}}

along
R
. In a more compact notation,
L:=\mathsf{Ran}_{R}\mathrm{id}_{\mathbf{B}}
— but
in this case we only know the action of
\mathsf{Ran}_{R}
on the object

\mathrm{id}_{\mathbf{B}}
, and we don’t know if this
\mathsf{Ran}_{R}
can be extended to a
“real” functor whose domain is the whole of
\mathbf{B}^{\mathbf{B}}
. The
diagram is:

\textstyle{GR}
\textstyle{\forall G}
\textstyle{GR}
\textstyle{LR}
\scriptstyle{UR}
\textstyle{\forall G}
\textstyle{L{:=}\mathsf{Ran}_{R}\mathrm{id}_{\mathbf{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\exists!U}

\textstyle{LR}
\textstyle{L{:=}\mathsf{Ran}_{R}\mathrm{id}_{\mathbf{B}}}
\textstyle{LR}
\textstyle{\mathrm{id}_{\mathbf{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}
\textstyle{GR}
\textstyle{\mathrm{id}_{\mathbf{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\forall V}
\textstyle{\mathbf{B}^{\mathbf{B}}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{B}^{\mathbf{A}}}
\scriptstyle{(\circ R)}
\textstyle{\mathbf{B}^{\mathbf{B}}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{B}^{\mathbf{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{Ran}_{R}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{B}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{A}}
\scriptstyle{R}

\textstyle{\mathbf{B}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{A}}
\scriptstyle{R}
\textstyle{\mathbf{A}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{B}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle L}
\textstyle{\mathbf{A}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{B}}
\scriptstyle{\forall G}
\textstyle{\mathbf{B}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{B}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}_{\mathbf{B}}}
\textstyle{{\Downarrow\scriptstyle\varepsilon}}
\textstyle{{\Downarrow\scriptstyle\exists!U}}

To show that this works we have to prove that
\forall V.\exists!U.(\varepsilon\cdot UR=V)
. We
will do that by “inverting the equation
\varepsilon\cdot UR=V
”:

\textstyle{\mathbf{A}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{B}}
\scriptstyle{L}
\textstyle{\mathbf{B}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{A}}
\scriptstyle{R}
\textstyle{\mathbf{A}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{B}}
\scriptstyle{L}
\textstyle{\mathbf{A}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{A}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}_{A}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{A}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{B}}
\scriptstyle{G}
\textstyle{\mathbf{B}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{B}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}_{B}}
\textstyle{{\Downarrow\scriptstyle\eta}}
\textstyle{{\Downarrow\scriptstyle U}}
\textstyle{{\Downarrow\scriptstyle\varepsilon}}
\textstyle{{=}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{A}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{B}}
\scriptstyle{L}
\textstyle{\mathbf{A}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{B}}
\scriptstyle{L}
\textstyle{\mathbf{A}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{A}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}_{A}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{A}}

\textstyle{\mathbf{B}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{B}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}_{B}}
\textstyle{{\Downarrow\scriptstyle U}}

\textstyle{\mathbf{A}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{B}}
\scriptstyle{L}
\textstyle{\mathbf{B}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{A}}
\scriptstyle{R}
\textstyle{\mathbf{A}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{A}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}_{A}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{A}}

\textstyle{\mathbf{B}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{B}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}_{B}}

\textstyle{{=\;VL\cdot\eta}}

The solution in
U:=VL\cdot G\eta
.

8.10
A formula for Kan extensions

The sections X.3 of
[
CWM
]
and 6.2 of
[
Riehl
]
discuss a
formula for calculating Kan extensions, that defines
\mathsf{Ran}_{F}H
as the
functor whose action on objects is:

B\mapsto{\operatorname{Lim}}(B{\downarrow}F\overset{\pi}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{A}\overset{H}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{Set}),

and its action on morphisms is “obvious” in the sense of Section

3
. I found this formula totally impossible to
understand until I finally found a way to visualize what it “meant”.

For each object
B\in\mathbf{B}
the functor
B{\downarrow}F\xrightarrow{H\circ\pi}\mathbf{Set}
can be
regarded as a diagram in
\mathbf{Set}
whose shape is the shape of the comma
category
B{\downarrow}F
. If
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
are finite preorder categories
and
F
is an inclusion then
B{\downarrow}F
can “inherit its shape” from

\mathbf{A}
; inclusions of preorders are “toy examples” “for
children”, but they give us some intuition to start with, and they
can help us understand the formal version that can handle more general
cases.

\mathsf{Ran}_{F}
as a right adjoint — note that
we use
\mathbf{Set}
instead of
\mathbf{C}
to make things less abstract,

and

3^{\prime}{\downarrow}F=\left(\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 13.949pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern 31.02628pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\phantom{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&3^{\prime}\\

and
(1^{\prime}{\downarrow}F\xrightarrow{H\circ\pi}\mathbf{Set})=\left(\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 9.80554pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern 20.17798pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{H_{2}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 20.17798pt\raise-44.97528pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{H_{6}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 26.98352pt\raise-22.32098pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.98352pt\raise-37.64197pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-9.80554pt\raise-44.97528pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{H_{5}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 20.17798pt\raise-44.97528pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{H_{6}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 11.99176pt\raise-44.97528pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 20.17798pt\raise-44.97528pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}}}}}\right)

and
(3^{\prime}{\downarrow}F\xrightarrow{H\circ\pi}\mathbf{Set})=\left(\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 9.80554pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern 20.17798pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\phantom{H_{2}}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-9.80554pt\raise-44.97528pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{H_{5}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 20.17798pt\raise-44.97528pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{H_{6}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 11.99176pt\raise-44.97528pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 20.17798pt\raise-44.97528pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}}}}}\right)
;

so
R(1^{\prime})={\operatorname{Lim}}(1^{\prime}{\downarrow}F\xrightarrow{H\circ\pi}\mathbf{Set})=H_{2}\times_{H_{6}}H_{5}
,

and
R(3^{\prime})={\operatorname{Lim}}(3^{\prime}{\downarrow}F\xrightarrow{H\circ\pi}\mathbf{Set})=H_{5}
.

We can follow the same pattern to calculate
R(2^{\prime})
,
R(4^{\prime})
,
R(5^{\prime})
,

R(6^{\prime})
.

The square of the adjunction becomes this, in this particular case:

\textstyle{GF}
\textstyle{G}
\textstyle{GF}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{G}
\textstyle{\mathsf{Ran}_{F}H}

\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{\mathsf{Ran}_{F}H}
\textstyle{{=R}}

8.11
Functors as objects

One way to treat a diagram in
\mathbf{Set}
like this

F\qquad:=\qquad\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 10.50002pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern 9.76128pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\{24,25\}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-10.50002pt\raise-29.98352pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\{1\}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 11.99176pt\raise-14.99176pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 8.0pt\raise-21.98352pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.76128pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\{24,25\}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 44.74481pt\raise-29.98352pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\{2,3\}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 44.97528pt\raise-3.7362pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\begin{smallmatrix}24\mapsto 2\\

as a functor is to think that that diagram is an abbreviation — it
is just the upper-right part of a diagram like this,

\textstyle{\left(\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-1.5,-0.5)\put(0.0,3.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${1}$\hss}}\put(-1.0,2.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${2}$\hss}}\put(1.0,2.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${3}$\hss}}\put(0.0,1.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${4}$\hss}}\put(0.0,0.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${5}$\hss}}\put(-0.5,2.5){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\swarrow}$\hss}}\put(0.5,2.5){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\searrow}$\hss}}\put(-0.5,1.5){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\searrow}$\hss}}\put(0.5,1.5){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\swarrow}$\hss}}\put(0.0,0.5){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\downarrow}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}\right)}

\textstyle{\mathbf{K}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Set}}
\scriptstyle{F}

where we add the extra hint that the index category
\mathbf{K}
is exactly
the kite-shaped preorder category drawn above the “
\mathbf{K}
”.

The convention (CFSh) says that the image by a functor of a diagram is
a diagram with the same shape, so according to that convention we have

F(1)=\{24,25\}
,
F(4\rightarrow 5)=(\{1\}\overset{1\mapsto 1}{\rightarrow}\{0,1\})
, and so
on; so the upper right part of the diagram above
defines

F
.

Note that the single ‘
\mapsto
’ above the
\mathbf{K}\overset{F}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{Set}
stands for
several ‘
\mapsto
’s, one for each object and one for each morphism, and
note that
F
is an object of
\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{K}}
.

8.12
Geometric morphisms for children

Let
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
be these preorder categories, and let

f:\mathbf{A}\rightarrow\mathbf{B}
be the inclusion functor from
\mathbf{A}
to
\mathbf{B}
:

A:=\left(\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-2.5,-0.5)\put(-2.0,2.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${2}$\hss}}\put(2.0,2.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${3}$\hss}}\put(-1.0,1.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\searrow}$\hss}}\put(1.0,1.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\swarrow}$\hss}}\put(3.0,1.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\searrow}$\hss}}\put(0.0,0.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${4}$\hss}}\put(4.0,0.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${5}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}\right)\qquad B:=\left(\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-4.5,-0.5)\put(-2.0,6.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${1}$\hss}}\put(-3.0,5.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\swarrow}$\hss}}\put(-1.0,5.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\searrow}$\hss}}\put(-4.0,4.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${2}$\hss}}\put(0.0,4.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${3}$\hss}}\put(-3.0,3.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\searrow}$\hss}}\put(-1.0,3.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\swarrow}$\hss}}\put(1.0,3.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\searrow}$\hss}}\put(-2.0,2.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${4}$\hss}}\put(2.0,2.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${5}$\hss}}\put(-1.0,1.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\searrow}$\hss}}\put(1.0,1.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\swarrow}$\hss}}\put(0.0,0.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${6}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}\right)

The left half of the diagram below is the standard definition of a
geometric morphism
f
from a topos
\mathcal{E}
to a topos
\mathcal{F}
. A
geometric morphism
f:\mathcal{E}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
is actually an adjunction
f^{*}\dashv f_{*}

plus the guarantee that
f^{*}:\mathcal{E}\leftarrow\mathcal{F}
preserves limits, which
is a condition slightly weaker than requiring that
f^{*}
has a left
adjoint. When that left adjoint exists it is denoted by
f^{!}
, and we
say that
f^{!}\dashv f^{*}\dashv f_{*}
is an
essential geometric morphism
. The
only non-standard thing about the diagram at the left below is that is
contains an internal view of the adjunction
f^{*}\dashv f_{*}
.

\textstyle{f^{*}G}
\textstyle{G}
\textstyle{f^{*}G}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{G}
\textstyle{f_{*}H}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{f_{*}H}

\textstyle{\mathcal{E}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{F}}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{E}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{F}}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}

\textstyle{\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{A}}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{B}}}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{A}}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{B}}}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{A}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{B}}
\scriptstyle{f}

The right half of the diagram is a particular case of the left half.
Its lower line,
\mathbf{A}\overset{f}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{B}
, does not exist in the left
half. The inclusion functor
f
induces adjunctions
f^{!}\dashv f^{*}\dashv f_{*}
as
this,

\textstyle{\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{A}}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{B}}}
\scriptstyle{f^{!}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{A}}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{B}}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle f^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{A}}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{B}}}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{A}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{B}}
\scriptstyle{f}

where
f^{*}
is easy to define and
f^{!}
and
f_{*}
not so much — the
standard way to define
f^{!}
and
f_{*}
is by Kan extensions.

The big square in the upper part of the diagram is an internal view of
the adjunction
f^{*}\dashv f_{*}
, with the functors
f^{*}G
,
G
,
H
, and

f_{*}H
being displayed as their internal views. We can choose the sets

G_{1},\ldots,G_{6}
and the morphisms between them arbitrarily, so this
is an internal view of an arbitrary functor
G:\mathbf{B}\rightarrow\mathbf{Set}
; and the
same for
H
.

The arrow
f^{*}G\leftarrow\mapsfromchar G
can be read as a definition for the action
of
f^{*}
on objects — it just erases some parts of the diagram —
and the arrow
H\mapsto f_{*}H
can be read as a definition for the
action of
f_{*}
on objects —
f_{*}
“reconstructs”
H_{1}
and
H_{6}

in a certain natural way. It is easy to reconstruct the actions of

f^{*}
and
f_{*}
on morphisms from just what is shown, and to
reconstruct the two directions of the bijection.

The big diagram above can be used 1) to convince categorists who are
not seasoned toposophers that this diagrammatic language can make some
difficult categorical concepts more accessible, and 2) as a starting
point to generate diagrams “for children” for several parts of the
Elephant (
[
Elephant
]
), and even to prove new theorems on
toposes. For more on (1), see
[
OL18
]
and

[
Och18
]
; for (2), see
[
MDE
]
.

9
Related and unrelated work

The diagrammatic language that I described here seems to be unrelated
to the ones in
[
CK17
]
and
[
Coe11
]
— that
describe
lots
of diagrammatic languages — and also unrelated
to
[
Mar14
]
. We lower the level of abstraction — see for
example Section
8.7
— while they (usually)
raise it.

I’ve taken an approach that is the opposite of
[
CW01
]

and
[
Các04
]
. Cáccamo and Winskel define a derivation system
that can only construct functors, natural transformations, etc, that
obey the expected naturality conditions, while we allow some kinds of
sloppinesses, like constructing something that looks like a functor
and pretending that it is a functor when it may not be. When I started
working on this diagrammatic language I had a companion derivation
system for it;
[
IDARCT
, Section 14]
mentions it briefly, but it
doesn’t show the introduction rules that create (proto)functors and
(proto)natural transformations and that allow being sloppy (“in the
syntactical world”). That derivation system was incomplete in all
senses — it even had “rules” that I knew how to apply in
particular cases but I didn’t know how to formalize.

Some of my excuses for allowing one to pretend that a functor is a
functor and leaving the verification to a second stage come from

[
Che04
]
. I learned a
lot
on how mathematicians use
intuition and diagrams from
[
Krö07
]
—
[
Krö18
]
is a
great summary — and
[
Cor04
]
, and they have helped me to
identify which characteristics of my diagrammatic language are very
unusual and may be new, and that deserve to be presented in detail.

Many of the first ideas for my diagrammatic language appeared when I
was reading
[
See83
]
,
[
See84
]
,
[
See87
]
,

[
Jac99
]
, and
[
BCS06
]
and trying to draw the “missing
diagrams” in those papers in both the original notation and in the
“archetypal case” (
[
IDARCT
, Section 16]
).

Many of the later ideas appeared when I was trying to understand
sheaves using a certain approach “for children” (
[
PH2
]
): I
learned how to draw diagrams showing a Grothendieck topology, its
corresponding Lawvere-Tierney topology, and its corresponding nucleus

in particular cases
, and I knew that
there had to be a way

(in the sense of
[
Che04
]
) to “lift” these diagrams of
the correspondences in particular cases to a diagram of the
correspondences in the general case… the details of this “lifting”
were hard to formalize, but missing details started to become clear
when I required the diagrams to be translatable to a pseudocode that
could be translated to Agda. At this moment
[
PH2
]
is still
incomplete, but some of the ideas in DL were motivated by its
conceptual holes.
